# Daily Summary: 2025-08-15

### Pruning Long Chain-of-Thought of Large Reasoning Models via Small-Scale Preference Optimization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10164v1)
- **Authors**: Bin Hong, Jiayu Liu, Zhenya Huang, Kai Zhang, Mengdi Zhang
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) have demonstrated strong performance on complex tasks through long Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning. However, their lengthy outputs increase computational costs and may lead to overthinking, raising challenges in balancing reasoning effectiveness and efficiency. Current methods for efficient reasoning often compromise reasoning quality or require extensive resources. This paper investigates efficient methods to reduce the generation length of LRMs. We analyze generation path distributions and filter generated trajectories through difficulty estimation. Subsequently, we analyze the convergence behaviors of the objectives of various preference optimization methods under a Bradley-Terry loss based framework. Based on the analysis, we propose Length Controlled Preference Optimization (LCPO) that directly balances the implicit reward related to NLL loss. LCPO can effectively learn length preference with limited data and training. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our approach significantly reduces the average output length by over 50\% across multiple benchmarks while maintaining the reasoning performance. Our work highlights the potential for computationally efficient approaches in guiding LRMs toward efficient reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of excessive length in the outputs of Large Reasoning Models (LRMs) that use Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning.  Long outputs increase computational costs and can lead to overthinking. The authors propose Length Controlled Preference Optimization (LCPO), a method that aims to reduce the output length of LRMs while preserving reasoning performance.  LCPO leverages a Bradley-Terry loss framework and balances the implicit reward related to negative log-likelihood (NLL) loss to learn length preferences with limited data and training. The paper filters generated reasoning trajectories based on estimated problem difficulty and empirically analyses the generation space. Experiments on math reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that LCPO can significantly reduce output length while maintaining reasoning accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper has some novelty in its approach to length control in LRMs, specifically in using a balanced preference optimization method and analyzing the convergence properties of different preference learning objectives. While other methods exist for shortening outputs, the paper's analysis and proposed LCPO framework offer a more nuanced approach than simply imposing length penalties or relying solely on inference-time pruning. The insight regarding the inherent negative reward of NLL loss is a valuable contribution.

* **Significance:** The significance lies in the potential for more efficient and computationally feasible reasoning with LRMs. Reducing output length is crucial for deploying these models in resource-constrained environments and for preventing overthinking on simpler tasks. While the experiments are primarily on math reasoning, the principles could potentially generalize to other reasoning-intensive tasks.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly identifies and motivates the problem of excessive length in LRM outputs.
    * **Analytical Approach:** The authors provide a theoretical analysis of different preference optimization objectives, leading to the design of LCPO. This analytical grounding strengthens the paper.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The experimental results on multiple benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of LCPO in reducing output length while maintaining accuracy. The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components.
    * **Efficient Training:** The paper highlights the efficiency of LCPO in terms of data requirements and training steps.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Generalization Evidence:** The core experiments are limited to math reasoning tasks. While MMLU demonstrates OOD generalization, experiments on other reasoning tasks (e.g., commonsense reasoning, question answering) would strengthen the claims.
    * **Dataset Bias:** The reliance on a specific training dataset (LIMR) could limit the generalizability of the findings. While the ablation study touches on this, exploring the performance with diverse training data would be beneficial.
    * **Implementation Details:** While hyperparameters are stated, more details on the exact implementation of the data filtering method (based on estimated difficulty) would be useful.
    * **Comparison to More Recent Methods:**  The baselines are solid but could be expanded to include comparisons against even more recent state-of-the-art methods in length control for LLMs *if applicable given the submission date* of the paper.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on efficient reasoning with LRMs. The analysis of preference optimization objectives and the design of LCPO could inspire new methods for length control and resource-efficient deployment of these models.  The emphasis on small-scale training is particularly relevant for researchers with limited computational resources.

* **Rigorous Rationale:**  The paper provides both empirical results and theoretical insights. However, the experimental scope could be broadened, and the claims of broad generalizability are not fully substantiated. The improvement is strong in math problems, but further validation is needed.

**Score: 6.5**

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field with a well-defined problem, a theoretically grounded approach, and empirical validation.  The reduction in output length while preserving reasoning accuracy is significant, and the efficient training requirements are attractive. However, the limited scope of the experiments, dataset reliance, and certain details of implementation slightly detract from the overall impact and prevent a higher score.  It is above average, but has some limitations that prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### Benchmark-Driven Selection of AI: Evidence from DeepSeek-R1
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10173v1)
- **Authors**: Petr Spelda, Vit Stritecky
- **Abstract**: Evaluation of reasoning language models gained importance after it was observed that they can combine their existing capabilities into novel traces of intermediate steps before task completion and that the traces can sometimes help them to generalize better than past models. As reasoning becomes the next scaling dimension of large language models, careful study of their capabilities in critical tasks is needed. We show that better performance is not always caused by test-time algorithmic improvements or model sizes but also by using impactful benchmarks as curricula for learning. We call this benchmark-driven selection of AI and show its effects on DeepSeek-R1 using our sequential decision-making problem from Humanity's Last Exam. Steering development of AI by impactful benchmarks trades evaluation for learning and makes novelty of test tasks key for measuring generalization capabilities of reasoning models. Consequently, some benchmarks could be seen as curricula for training rather than unseen test sets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the "benchmark-driven selection" of AI, focusing on DeepSeek-R1 and its performance on a sequential decision-making task from Humanity's Last Exam (HLE). The authors argue that improved performance in large language models (LLMs) is not solely due to algorithmic advancements or increased model size but also to the models being trained or fine-tuned on impactful benchmarks. This benchmark-driven selection acts as a curriculum for learning, potentially turning evaluation tasks into training data.  The authors compare DeepSeek-R1 before and after its inclusion in HLE, along with Phi-4-r+, to demonstrate how inclusion in a widely used benchmark can affect model performance. They observe that the later version of DeepSeek-R1 (DS-R1-0528) outperforms the earlier one and Phi-4-r+ on the task, and that the pre-HLE release of DeepSeek-R1 performs significantly worse, indicating benchmark-driven learning. They conclude that the impact of benchmarks needs to be carefully considered when evaluating LLMs, and that careful choice of benchmarks and test datasets is key to measuring generalization capabilities. The paper suggests that treating some benchmarks as curricula rather than strictly as unseen test sets offers a more accurate view of the model's development.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core concept of "benchmark-driven selection" is interesting, but not entirely novel. The phenomenon of models overfitting to benchmarks or incorporating benchmark data during training is a well-known issue in machine learning. The paper’s contribution lies in articulating this in the context of large language models and arguing that it is increasingly problematic given the scale and rapid iteration of LLM development and the fact that even seemingly "unseen" benchmarks can influence the training process.

*   **Significance:**  The paper raises important questions about how we evaluate LLMs and interpret benchmark results. If benchmarks are implicitly becoming part of the training data, then the apparent generalization capabilities of models might be overestimated. This has implications for the field's understanding of model progress and the trustworthiness of AI systems. It is especially significant considering the use of RLHF, which relies on human preferences which may be influenced by benchmarks. Further, the paper addresses the recent debate of data contamination in LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper is clearly written and well-structured.
    *   The focus on DeepSeek-R1 and its publicly available versions provides a concrete example to illustrate the benchmark-driven selection phenomenon.
    *   The experimental design, while simple, provides supporting evidence for the argument.
    *   The discussion is thorough, addressing the implications for the field.
    *   Human evaluation adds to the paper's strengths due to AI's limited capacity to recognize concepts.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The empirical evidence is limited to a single task and a small number of models.  The conclusions could be strengthened by including more tasks and models. While the authors do acknowledge that fact in the conclusion, it does weaken the conclusions of the paper.
    *   The claim that benchmark-driven selection "trades evaluation for learning" is somewhat strong.  It is more accurate to say that it *blurs the line* between evaluation and learning, rather than entirely replacing one with the other.  The effect is likely complex.
    *   The paper does not address the question of how to *prevent* benchmark-driven selection, other than suggesting the need for disposable tasks and independent community stewardship of difficult tasks. More concrete suggestions could enhance the practical value of the paper.
    *   The connection to U.S. export controls, while a plausible contextual factor, isn't directly demonstrated and could be seen as speculative.
    *   The paper does not test other tests to see if DeepSeek-R1 had a similar performance boost. Testing several other tests could add to the claims of the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could prompt a more critical approach to LLM evaluation, encouraging researchers to consider the potential for benchmark-driven selection and develop methods to mitigate its effects. It could influence the design of new benchmarks, emphasizing the need for truly unseen tasks and more robust evaluation methodologies.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper addresses an important and timely issue, but its limitations in empirical scope and specific solutions prevent it from achieving a higher score. While the core concept is interesting, it's not entirely groundbreaking. The paper's strength is in its clear articulation of the problem in the context of modern LLM development and its thought-provoking discussion of the implications. The limitations (single task, limited number of models) prevent the paper from showing that the benchmark-driven selection phenomenon is broadly applicable, though it does raise awareness on potential pitfals when comparing LLMs.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 6/10

### Efficient Forward-Only Data Valuation for Pretrained LLMs and VLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10180v1)
- **Authors**: Wenlong Deng, Jiaming Zhang, Qi Zeng, Christos Thrampoulidis, Boying Gong, Xiaoxiao Li
- **Abstract**: Quantifying the influence of individual training samples is essential for enhancing the transparency and accountability of large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs). However, existing data valuation methods often rely on Hessian information or model retraining, making them computationally prohibitive for billion-parameter models. In this work, we introduce For-Value, a forward-only data valuation framework that enables scalable and efficient influence estimation for both LLMs and VLMs. By leveraging the rich representations of modern foundation models, For-Value computes influence scores using a simple closed-form expression based solely on a single forward pass, thereby eliminating the need for costly gradient computations. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that For-Value accurately estimates per-sample influence by capturing alignment in hidden representations and prediction errors between training and validation samples. Extensive experiments show that For-Value matches or outperforms gradient-based baselines in identifying impactful fine-tuning examples and effectively detecting mislabeled data.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces For-Value, a novel forward-only data valuation framework designed for pretrained large language models (LLMs) and vision-language models (VLMs). Unlike existing data valuation methods that rely on computationally expensive Hessian information or model retraining, For-Value estimates the influence of each training sample based on its contribution to improving the likelihood of validation data using a single forward pass. The method leverages the rich hidden representations of pretrained models and formulates a closed-form expression capturing representation similarity and prediction error alignment between training and validation samples. The authors provide a theoretical analysis justifying their approach and demonstrate empirically that For-Value matches or outperforms gradient-based baselines in identifying impactful fine-tuning examples and detecting mislabeled data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the **forward-only** approach to data valuation for LLMs and VLMs.  Prior methods often require access to gradients or model weights, which are often unavailable or computationally expensive to obtain. For-Value's reliance on a single forward pass is a significant departure from these methods and represents a substantial practical advantage.  The idea of using hidden representations and the alignment with prediction errors is conceptually interesting, although the underlying principles of representation similarity have been explored in other contexts.  The specific combination and application to efficient data valuation for LLMs/VLMs is novel.

*   **Significance:** Data valuation is increasingly important for understanding and improving the behavior of large models.  Being able to identify influential or noisy training data is crucial for fairness, bias mitigation, and overall model reliability. The significance of For-Value stems from its scalability and efficiency.  Existing methods are often prohibitive for billion-parameter models. The authors demonstrate that For-Value enables data valuation on larger models (up to 72B parameters) where traditional methods are impractical. The ability to detect mislabeled data and identify crucial fine-tuning examples efficiently has direct implications for model development and deployment. However, the practical impact is somewhat constrained by the fact that it still needs a validation dataset representative of the target domain.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Efficiency:** The primary strength is the computational efficiency of the forward-only approach. The paper effectively highlights the speed gains compared to gradient-based baselines.
    *   **Theoretical Justification:** The paper provides a clear theoretical analysis linking the proposed measure to the dynamics of the learning process.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** The experiments are comprehensive and demonstrate the effectiveness of For-Value across various tasks, model sizes, and datasets.  The comparison against multiple baselines strengthens the validity of the results.
    *   **Scalability:** The paper clearly shows that For-Value performs well from 1.5B up to 72B models, while other methods fail to scale up.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependence on Hidden Representations:** The method's performance hinges on the quality of the hidden representations produced by the pretrained model.  While the authors assume "unconstrained features," the effectiveness could be limited if the representations are not sufficiently informative for the downstream task. This could limit its effectiveness on different pre-trained architectures.
    *   **Reliance on Validation Data:** Like other data valuation approaches, For-Value requires a validation dataset. The quality and representativeness of the validation data will significantly influence the accuracy of the valuation. If the validation data is biased or not representative, the identified "influential" training samples might not be beneficial for the true target distribution.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Failure Cases:** While the paper presents compelling results, a more detailed analysis of failure cases and situations where For-Value might underperform would be valuable. Understanding the limitations of the method would provide a more complete picture of its applicability.
    *   **Comparison Methods limitations**: Although they tried to compare with other methods such as TracIn, it is difficult to apply on the LLMs setting since they have a high memory footprint.
*   **Potential Impact:** For-Value has the potential to become a widely used data valuation technique for LLMs and VLMs, particularly in scenarios where access to gradients or model weights is restricted. The simplicity and efficiency of the method could encourage broader adoption of data valuation practices in model development and deployment pipelines.

**Overall Score Justification:**

Considering the novelty of the forward-only approach, the significance of efficient data valuation for large models, the comprehensive empirical validation, and the practical advantages of For-Value, but also acknowledging the limitations related to hidden representation quality and validation data dependence, I would assign the following score:

Score: 7.5

The score reflects a solid and significant contribution to the field. While the underlying idea of representation similarity is not entirely new, the specific application to efficient data valuation for LLMs/VLMs using a forward-only approach is novel and practically valuable. The paper's theoretical grounding and empirical validation strengthen its credibility. The method clearly outperforms the baseline and the code and analysis appears well written. The weaknesses related to the dependence on the quality of the hidden representations and the dependence on having access to a validation set are limitations to consider, but do not detract from the overall contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### PakBBQ: A Culturally Adapted Bias Benchmark for QA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10186v1)
- **Authors**: Abdullah Hashmat, Muhammad Arham Mirza, Agha Ali Raza
- **Abstract**: With the widespread adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) across various applications, it is empirical to ensure their fairness across all user communities. However, most LLMs are trained and evaluated on Western centric data, with little attention paid to low-resource languages and regional contexts. To address this gap, we introduce PakBBQ, a culturally and regionally adapted extension of the original Bias Benchmark for Question Answering (BBQ) dataset. PakBBQ comprises over 214 templates, 17180 QA pairs across 8 categories in both English and Urdu, covering eight bias dimensions including age, disability, appearance, gender, socio-economic status, religious, regional affiliation, and language formality that are relevant in Pakistan. We evaluate multiple multilingual LLMs under both ambiguous and explicitly disambiguated contexts, as well as negative versus non negative question framings. Our experiments reveal (i) an average accuracy gain of 12\% with disambiguation, (ii) consistently stronger counter bias behaviors in Urdu than in English, and (iii) marked framing effects that reduce stereotypical responses when questions are posed negatively. These findings highlight the importance of contextualized benchmarks and simple prompt engineering strategies for bias mitigation in low resource settings.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PakBBQ, a culturally adapted bias benchmark for question answering (QA) specifically tailored to the Pakistani context. PakBBQ extends the original BBQ dataset, covering 17180 QA pairs across 8 bias dimensions (age, disability, appearance, gender, socio-economic status, religion, regional affiliation, and language formality) in both English and Urdu. The authors evaluate several multilingual LLMs on PakBBQ, examining overall accuracy, bias disparity, and performance based on context, polarity, and template type. Their findings highlight stereotypical biases, particularly concerning gender identity and socio-economic status, and show that disambiguation and negative framing can reduce stereotypical responses. The work also exposes performance disparities between English and Urdu, and assesses the challenges of cultural adaptation, leading to better understanding of regional bias.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Addresses a significant gap:**  The paper tackles a critical issue in NLP – the lack of localized bias benchmarks for low-resource languages and non-Western cultural contexts. This is essential for ensuring fairness and mitigating potential harm from LLM deployment in these regions.
    *   **Comprehensive dataset:** PakBBQ appears to be a well-constructed and comprehensive dataset, thoughtfully designed to capture Pakistan-specific biases, including previously unaddressed biases like regional bias and language formality. The annotation process with the aim of cultural relevance and quality control is well-explained.
    *   **Systematic analysis:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation of several LLMs, using multiple metrics and considering various factors like context, polarity, and template type, leading to valuable insights. The analysis of both ambiguous and disambiguated contexts reveals important information about the model's reliance on stereotypes.
    *   **Actionable findings:** The findings regarding the effectiveness of disambiguation and negative framing as bias mitigation techniques are practical and actionable, especially for low-resource settings where fine-tuning may be limited.
    *   **Multilingual focus:**  By including both English and Urdu, the work highlights cross-linguistic performance disparities and the challenges of applying LLMs in low-resource languages.
    *   **Transparent and Replicable:** The data set is released on PakBBQ, promoting future researches to use the dataset.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Linguistic Coverage:** The dataset currently covers only Urdu and English scripts, neglecting other significant regional languages in Pakistan. The conclusion admits this limitation, implying there is room for further work.
    *   **Dependence on Machine Translation:** The use of Google Translate API may introduce translation artifacts and affect the accuracy of Urdu responses, impacting the reliability of cross-lingual comparisons. While this approach is justified, it is a source of potential error that could influence outcomes and may require more critical assessment.
    *   **Zero-Shot Prompting:**  While zero-shot evaluation is valuable, it may not fully represent the performance of LLMs when fine-tuned or adapted for the specific cultural context.
    *   **Counter Bias:** The study shows that models have strong inclination towards counter bias, and therefore, further work on how to develop approprite cultural sensitivity can be assessed.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The creation of a culturally adapted bias benchmark for a low-resource language and a non-Western context is a novel and important contribution. This work moves beyond simple translation and addresses the unique challenges of bias evaluation in such settings. The findings regarding cultural-specific biases, language formality, and counter bias tendencies, are valuable additions to the field of fairness in NLP.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a crucial gap in bias evaluation for low-resource languages and non-Western contexts. While there are limitations, like the use of only one translation API, and no tests on fine tuned models, the strengths, including a new dataset, comprehensive analysis, and actionable findings, outweigh the weaknesses. The work has the potential to influence future research in culturally sensitive NLP and promote the development of more equitable and responsible AI systems. It also presents methods and findings that other researchers can adapt to other cultural contexts.

**Score: 7.5**
- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt-Response Semantic Divergence Metrics for Faithfulness Hallucination and Misalignment Detection in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10192v1)
- **Authors**: Igor Halperin
- **Abstract**: The proliferation of Large Language Models (LLMs) is challenged by hallucinations, critical failure modes where models generate non-factual, nonsensical or unfaithful text. This paper introduces Semantic Divergence Metrics (SDM), a novel lightweight framework for detecting Faithfulness Hallucinations -- events of severe deviations of LLMs responses from input contexts. We focus on a specific implementation of these LLM errors, {confabulations, defined as responses that are arbitrary and semantically misaligned with the user's query. Existing methods like Semantic Entropy test for arbitrariness by measuring the diversity of answers to a single, fixed prompt. Our SDM framework improves upon this by being more prompt-aware: we test for a deeper form of arbitrariness by measuring response consistency not only across multiple answers but also across multiple, semantically-equivalent paraphrases of the original prompt. Methodologically, our approach uses joint clustering on sentence embeddings to create a shared topic space for prompts and answers. A heatmap of topic co-occurances between prompts and responses can be viewed as a quantified two-dimensional visualization of the user-machine dialogue. We then compute a suite of information-theoretic metrics to measure the semantic divergence between prompts and responses. Our practical score, $\mathcal{S}_H$, combines the Jensen-Shannon divergence and Wasserstein distance to quantify this divergence, with a high score indicating a Faithfulness hallucination. Furthermore, we identify the KL divergence KL(Answer $||$ Prompt) as a powerful indicator of \textbf{Semantic Exploration}, a key signal for distinguishing different generative behaviors. These metrics are further combined into the Semantic Box, a diagnostic framework for classifying LLM response types, including the dangerous, confident confabulation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Semantic Divergence Metrics (SDM), a novel framework for detecting faithfulness hallucinations (severe deviations of LLM responses from input contexts, specifically confabulations) in large language models (LLMs). SDM improves upon existing methods like Semantic Entropy by being more "prompt-aware." It tests for arbitrariness by measuring response consistency across multiple, semantically-equivalent paraphrases of the original prompt. The approach utilizes joint clustering on sentence embeddings to create a shared topic space for prompts and answers, visualizing the user-machine dialogue via a heatmap of topic co-occurrences. Information-theoretic metrics, including a combined Jensen-Shannon divergence and Wasserstein distance score (SH), and KL divergence KL(Answer || Prompt) are used to quantify semantic divergence.  These metrics are incorporated into a diagnostic framework, the Semantic Box, for classifying LLM response types, including confident confabulation. The paper emphasizes real-time detection of faithfulness hallucinations and semantic misalignment in black-box LLMs, for both single-query and multi-turn scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Prompt Awareness:** The paper's key contribution is its emphasis on prompt awareness. By incorporating paraphrased prompts and jointly clustering prompt and answer embeddings, it moves beyond simpler methods that treat the prompt as a static context.
    *   **Comprehensive Metrics:** SDM uses a suite of information-theoretic and distributional metrics, offering a more granular and multi-faceted assessment than single-metric approaches. The combination of Ensemble JSD and Wasserstein distance into the SH score, along with the KL divergence, captures different facets of semantic divergence.
    *   **Visual Diagnostic (Semantic Box):**  The Semantic Box offers a visually intuitive framework for classifying LLM response behaviors, highlighting the nuances between different modes of success and failure (e.g., separating creative responses from confident confabulations).
    *   **Controlled Experiments:** The experiments, especially the one with a controlled "stability gradient," effectively demonstrate the framework's sensitivity to varying degrees of creative freedom and potential instability.
    *   **Practicality:** Designed for a black-box setting, the method can be readily applied to existing LLMs without requiring internal access.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity of Clustering:** Hierarchical clustering is the most computationally intensive part of the process, with O(S^2) time complexity. For large interaction histories, this might become a bottleneck. Although the text claims this is manageable when S is in the hundreds, more practical discussion about the scalability of the chosen embeddings and clustering algorithm for real world scenarios might be required.

    *   **Calibration Dependence:** The method's effectiveness relies on the quality of the initial sentence embeddings, Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B in this case, and on the ability to choose appropriate weighting coefficients for combining different measures, such as the combination of JSD and Wasserstein to make the SH score. The work suggests that for deployment in a domain, it must be calibrated by comparing the SDM analysis to a dataset with semantic deviation or hallucination labels.

    *   **Limited Scope of Experiments:** The experiments, although informative, are somewhat limited in scale and diversity of prompts and models tested. The paper only tests the gpt-40 model. The generalizability of the framework's performance across a wider range of LLMs and tasks requires further validation.

    *   **Theoretical vs. Practical Grounding:** While the information-theoretic foundation is well-articulated, the practical implementation (e.g., the choice of specific metrics and weights) seems somewhat empirically driven. A deeper theoretical justification for these specific choices could strengthen the paper.

    *   **Reliance on Paraphrasing Quality:**  The success of SDM hinges on the quality and diversity of the paraphrases generated for the initial prompt. Poor paraphrases might not adequately probe the LLM's response stability, leading to inaccurate results.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The paper provides a novel and practical approach to hallucination detection. The use of paraphrasing, joint embedding clustering, and a combination of information-theoretic measures in a prompt-aware manner is a significant advancement over existing methods. The diagnostic framework (Semantic Box) offers a valuable tool for understanding and classifying LLM behaviors. The paper's findings have implications for building more reliable and interpretable evaluation systems for deployed language models, as well as enhancing the safety and trustworthiness of LLM-powered applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper addresses a critical challenge in the field of LLMs (hallucination detection) with a novel and well-reasoned framework. While it has some limitations, such as the computational complexity of clustering and the reliance on paraphrase quality, the strengths (prompt awareness, comprehensive metrics, visual diagnostic, controlled experiments, and practicality) outweigh the weaknesses. The paper's contributions are significant and have the potential to influence future research in this area. The novelty lies in the unique combination of prompt paraphrasing, joint embedding, and multi-faceted metric analysis within the context of faithfulness hallucination detection.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### B-repLer: Semantic B-rep Latent Editor using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10201v1)
- **Authors**: Yilin Liu, Niladri Shekhar Dutt, Changjian Li, Niloy J. Mitra
- **Abstract**: Multimodal large language models (mLLMs), trained in a mixed modal setting as a universal model, have been shown to compete with or even outperform many specialized algorithms for imaging and graphics tasks. As demonstrated across many applications, mLLMs' ability to jointly process image and text data makes them suitable for zero-shot applications or efficient fine-tuning towards specialized tasks. However, they have had limited success in 3D analysis and editing tasks. This is due to both the lack of suitable (annotated) 3D data as well as the idiosyncrasies of 3D representations. In this paper, we investigate whether mLLMs can be adapted to support high-level editing of Boundary Representation (B-rep) CAD objects. B-reps remain the industry-standard for precisely encoding engineering objects, but are challenging as the representation is fragile (i.e. can easily lead to invalid CAD objects) and no publicly available data source exists with semantically-annotated B-reps or CAD construction history. We present B-repLer as a finetuned mLLM that can understand text prompts and make semantic edits on given B-Reps to produce valid outputs. We enable this via a novel multimodal architecture, specifically designed to handle B-rep models, and demonstrate how existing CAD tools, in conjunction with mLLMs, can be used to automatically generate the required reasoning dataset, without relying on external annotations. We extensively evaluate B-repLer and demonstrate several text-based B-rep edits of various complexity, which were not previously possible.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "B-repLer: Semantic B-rep Latent Editor using Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces B-repLer, a novel approach for editing Boundary Representation (B-rep) CAD models using natural language instructions.  B-repLer leverages a multimodal large language model (mLLM) to interpret high-level user prompts and perform semantic edits directly in the B-rep latent space.  The method tackles the challenges of B-rep editing, including the fragility of the representation, the lack of semantically annotated B-rep datasets, and the difficulty of interpreting abstract editing instructions. B-repLer employs a two-stage architecture: 1) a fine-tuned mLLM localizes the modification region and translates user prompts into detailed geometric instructions, and 2) a bespoke transformer operates within the B-rep latent space to perform the edit while preserving B-rep validity.  The authors also introduce BrepEDIT-10K, a procedurally generated dataset of paired B-rep models with semantic annotations to train and evaluate their approach. Experiments demonstrate B-repLer's ability to perform various text-based B-rep edits, outperforming a baseline method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a significant step forward in CAD model editing by directly manipulating B-rep representations through natural language instructions. While previous works have explored CAD generation and editing with LLMs, B-repLer's focus on native B-rep editing, without relying on intermediate representations or CAD program sequences, is novel. Using B-rep latent space is another smart method which is helpful for keeping CAD models valid and useful for editing. The two-stage approach is also clever, making task more manageable. The introduction of the BrepEDIT-10K dataset is a valuable contribution, addressing the lack of semantically annotated B-rep data.
*   **Significance:** B-repLer addresses a crucial gap in CAD modeling, making it more accessible and intuitive. The ability to edit B-rep models with natural language opens up new possibilities for design exploration and collaboration. The method's ability to handle abstract instructions and maintain B-rep validity is particularly important for practical applications. The data annotation method is also important since it can be reused for other purposes.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Direct B-rep editing:**  Avoids the limitations of intermediate representations and CAD program sequences.
    *   **Semantic understanding:** Interprets high-level user prompts and generates detailed geometric instructions.
    *   **B-rep validity:** Preserves the integrity and correctness of the CAD model during editing.
    *   **Dataset generation:** Introduces a valuable dataset for text-based B-rep editing.
    *   **Two-Stage Pipeline:** Improves interpretability and accuracy.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited editing operations:** The current implementation supports only delete and add operations.  More complex editing operations (e.g., boolean operations, feature transformations) would enhance the method's versatility.
    *   **Dataset limitations:** The BrepEDIT-10K dataset is procedurally generated, which may limit its realism and diversity compared to real-world CAD models and editing scenarios. Also, single-step operations are not as practical as real CAD design where multiple steps are involved.
    *   **Validity ratio:** The validity ratio is not perfect, suggesting that some edits still lead to broken B-rep models.
    *   **Instruction Translation:** There is no perfect translation from instruction to final CAD model.
    *   **Lack of physical reasoning:** The edits are purely geometry-based, without considering physical constraints or performance criteria.
*   **Potential Influence:** B-repLer has the potential to significantly impact CAD modeling and design workflows. It can enable new forms of human-computer interaction, democratize CAD editing for non-experts, and facilitate the integration of LLMs into CAD software.  The BrepEDIT-10K dataset can serve as a benchmark for future research in text-based CAD editing.
* **Justification:**
The paper makes a significant contribution by proposing a new and effective approach for editing B-rep CAD models with natural language instructions. The method's direct manipulation of B-rep representations, semantic understanding, and preservation of B-rep validity are notable strengths. Although there are limitations in terms of supported editing operations and dataset realism, the paper presents a promising research direction with the potential to significantly impact the field of CAD modeling and design. It tackles a challenging problem with a creative solution.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.GR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Using Large Language Models to Measure Symptom Severity in Patients At Risk for Schizophrenia
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10226v1)
- **Authors**: Andrew X. Chen, Guillermo Horga, Sean Escola
- **Abstract**: Patients who are at clinical high risk (CHR) for schizophrenia need close monitoring of their symptoms to inform appropriate treatments. The Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale (BPRS) is a validated, commonly used research tool for measuring symptoms in patients with schizophrenia and other psychotic disorders; however, it is not commonly used in clinical practice as it requires a lengthy structured interview. Here, we utilize large language models (LLMs) to predict BPRS scores from clinical interview transcripts in 409 CHR patients from the Accelerating Medicines Partnership Schizophrenia (AMP-SCZ) cohort. Despite the interviews not being specifically structured to measure the BPRS, the zero-shot performance of the LLM predictions compared to the true assessment (median concordance: 0.84, ICC: 0.73) approaches human inter- and intra-rater reliability. We further demonstrate that LLMs have substantial potential to improve and standardize the assessment of CHR patients via their accuracy in assessing the BPRS in foreign languages (median concordance: 0.88, ICC: 0.70), and integrating longitudinal information in a one-shot or few-shot learning approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential of large language models (LLMs) to measure symptom severity in individuals at clinical high risk (CHR) for schizophrenia. The authors used LLMs to predict Brief Psychiatric Rating Scale (BPRS) scores from clinical interview transcripts of 409 CHR patients from the AMP-SCZ cohort. They found that the LLM's zero-shot performance, particularly when using transcripts from semi-structured interviews (PSYCHS), achieved concordance and ICC values approaching human inter- and intra-rater reliability. The LLM also demonstrated capabilities in assessing symptom severity from foreign language transcripts and integrating longitudinal information to improve prediction accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Application to a clinically relevant problem:**  The paper addresses a significant need in psychiatry: the efficient and standardized assessment of symptom severity in individuals at risk for schizophrenia.
    *   **Zero-shot learning:**  The use of LLMs in a zero-shot setting (without task-specific training) demonstrates a powerful ability to generalize from existing knowledge.
    *   **Foreign language assessment:** Demonstrating the LLM's ability to process and assess symptom severity from non-English transcripts is a valuable feature for multi-national studies and clinical settings.
    *   **Longitudinal integration:** Exploring the use of previous interviews and scores to improve accuracy is a promising approach for tracking symptom trajectories and personalizing assessments.
    *   **Rigorous Analysis:** The paper uses established statistical methods (concordance, ICC, RMSE, bootstrapping) to evaluate the performance of the LLM.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on BPRS as a gold standard:**  The BPRS, while widely used, is subjective and susceptible to rater bias.  Using it as the sole gold standard limits the validity of the LLM's evaluation. The reliability of human ratings on the BPRS may be problematic.
    *   **Nature of the Input Data:** The paper emphasizes the LLM's strong performance even though the provided interview transcripts (PSYCHS or unstructured) were not specifically designed to directly assess the BPRS.  This lack of "ground truth" in the input data could be viewed as a limitation. Better scores from the LLMs on semi-structured PSYCHs interview transcripts might be expected due to their more systematic nature.
    *   **Limited longitudinal data:** The study only had a small subset of patients with three or more time points, restricting the generalizability of the longitudinal analysis.
    *   **Prompt sensitivity:** Prompt engineering is still an inexact science and this paper notes sensitivity to prompts, so the reported results may be somewhat fragile.
    *   **Lack of comparison to other automated methods:** The study doesn't compare the LLM's performance to other existing automated methods for symptom assessment (e.g., machine learning models trained on specific linguistic features).

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in its application of LLMs to the specific domain of psychosis risk assessment, particularly in a zero-shot manner and for multilingual processing. While previous studies have explored LLMs for other psychiatric conditions, this work extends the application to a challenging clinical population (CHR individuals) and leverages a unique dataset (AMP-SCZ). The findings suggest that LLMs have the potential to improve the efficiency and standardization of symptom assessment in CHR individuals, which could ultimately lead to earlier and more effective interventions. The use of longitudinal data is a plus, and it would have been interesting to see if the LLMs could generate predictions of psychosis onset. The results are not especially strong with an ICC of 0.73 using PSYCH transcripts. There are also the usual difficulties associated with LLM experiments. A better approach would likely be a fine-tuned rather than a zero-shot model.

The approach is not completely new, but the paper makes progress in a new and interesting domain.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 6

**Justification:**

The paper represents a worthwhile contribution to the field of computational psychiatry. While the zero-shot approach demonstrates the power of LLMs, it's likely that fine-tuning an LLM on a specific CHR dataset would yield even better results. The paper is significant in that it highlights the potential for these kinds of models to be practically implemented. The limitations regarding the gold standard and amount of longitudinal data, prompt sensitivity, and lack of comparison with other methods, temper enthusiasm. The study makes incremental, but potentially valuable, progress. The paper is well-written and the findings are clearly presented.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Can Transformers Break Encryption Schemes via In-Context Learning?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10235v1)
- **Authors**: Jathin Korrapati, Patrick Mendoza, Aditya Tomar, Abein Abraham
- **Abstract**: In-context learning (ICL) has emerged as a powerful capability of transformer-based language models, enabling them to perform tasks by conditioning on a small number of examples presented at inference time, without any parameter updates. Prior work has shown that transformers can generalize over simple function classes like linear functions, decision trees, even neural networks, purely from context, focusing on numerical or symbolic reasoning over underlying well-structured functions. Instead, we propose a novel application of ICL into the domain of cryptographic function learning, specifically focusing on ciphers such as mono-alphabetic substitution and Vigen\`ere ciphers, two classes of private-key encryption schemes. These ciphers involve a fixed but hidden bijective mapping between plain text and cipher text characters. Given a small set of (cipher text, plain text) pairs, the goal is for the model to infer the underlying substitution and decode a new cipher text word. This setting poses a structured inference challenge, which is well-suited for evaluating the inductive biases and generalization capabilities of transformers under the ICL paradigm. Code is available at https://github.com/adistomar/CS182-project.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper "Can Transformers Break Encryption Schemes via In-Context Learning?". Here's a summary and a critical evaluation:

**Summary**

The paper explores the ability of Transformer-based language models (LLMs) to learn and break private-key encryption schemes *in-context* (ICL).  The authors focus on mono-alphabetic substitution and Vigenère ciphers. They train a Transformer model to predict plaintext characters given a prompt containing (ciphertext, plaintext) pairs and a new ciphertext character. The goal is to see if the model can infer the underlying decryption key without explicit training on that key, effectively meta-learning decryption. The authors compare the model's performance against classical cryptanalytic techniques like frequency analysis. Their results show that Transformers can achieve high accuracy in deciphering mono-alphabetic substitution ciphers and partial success in Vigenère ciphers, suggesting an ability to learn structured mappings. The paper also investigates the impact of training and test data distributions, key lengths, and model architecture on ICL performance in the context of cryptography.

**Critical Evaluation: Novelty and Significance**

This paper presents a novel application of in-context learning (ICL) to the field of cryptography. While previous works have explored the capabilities of Transformers as cryptanalysts via training directly on cipher text or with fine-tuning, this work uniquely focuses on the ability of LLMs to *learn* a private-key encryption scheme solely through a few ciphertext-plaintext pairs presented in a prompt, without parameter updates, mirroring the ICL approach commonly used for function class learning.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novelty of Application:**  Applying ICL to private-key cryptography is a fresh perspective. It is significantly different than training to just recognize ciphertexts (e.g. Dani et al. 2024) or fine-tuning a LLM for general cipher breaking (Li et al., 2025).
    *   **Well-Defined Experimental Setup:** The paper establishes a clear methodology for training and evaluating the models, including appropriate baselines (naive decoder, frequency analysis).
    *   **Structured Analysis:** The paper systematically investigates several factors, including the number of in-context examples, the distribution of the input data (natural language vs. random), and the complexity of the encryption scheme (mono-alphabetic vs. Vigenère, fixed key length vs. variable key length). This allows for a nuanced understanding of the capabilities and limitations of the ICL approach.
    *   **Bridging Meta-Learning and Cryptography:** The paper effectively connects the ICL literature on function class learning (e.g., Garg et al., 2022) with the field of neural cryptanalysis, offering new insights into the inductive biases and generalization abilities of Transformers.
    * **Useful negative results:** The limitations of the model with the Vigenere cipher provides the starting point for future work in improving architecture, context length, or training.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope of Encryption Schemes:** The paper only examines relatively simple classical ciphers (mono-alphabetic substitution and Vigenère). While these schemes serve as a good starting point, their simplicity limits the generalizability of the findings to more complex, modern cryptographic algorithms. The conclusion states there are limits to ICL in crypto. This is certainly true and this paper could be expanded to include modern, more complex ciphers, to further prove that point.
    *   **Model Size and Architecture:** The Transformer model used is relatively small (9.5M parameters). It's possible that larger, more powerful models could exhibit stronger ICL abilities and overcome some of the limitations observed in the Vigenère cipher experiments. The architecture is that of GPT2, so is relatively standard (but perhaps could be the reason ICL is possible in this domain).
    *   **Practical Cryptanalytic Value:**  The practical cryptanalytic value of this approach is currently limited. While the paper demonstrates that Transformers can learn decryption rules in-context, the performance is not always competitive with classical cryptanalytic techniques, especially for the Vigenère cipher with longer keys.
    * **The models are too small:** The experiments might be limited because there isn't an ability to extrapolate and learn without the limitations of small model size, for example.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **New Research Direction:**  The paper opens up a new research direction at the intersection of ICL and cryptography. It raises interesting questions about the potential for LLMs to learn and generalize cryptographic functions, potentially influencing the design and analysis of future cryptographic systems.
    *   **Improved understanding of ICL:**  The work also provides new understanding of how LLMs perform ICL with structured tasks.

**Overall Assessment:**

While the encryption schemes studied are relatively simple, the paper's novel application of ICL, well-defined experimental setup, and structured analysis contribute significantly to our understanding of the capabilities and limitations of Transformers in the context of cryptography. The paper opens up a new research avenue and raises important questions about the potential for LLMs to learn and generalize cryptographic functions, and provides better understanding of the internal components of ICL.

Score: 7
The paper presents an incremental, but significant, advance that will likely influence future research. The negative results are important to the overall story in that there are limitations with more complex ciphers, as well.
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Pruning and Malicious Injection: A Retraining-Free Backdoor Attack on Transformer Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10243v1)
- **Authors**: Taibiao Zhao, Mingxuan Sun, Hao Wang, Xiaobing Chen, Xiangwei Zhou
- **Abstract**: Transformer models have demonstrated exceptional performance and have become indispensable in computer vision (CV) and natural language processing (NLP) tasks. However, recent studies reveal that transformers are susceptible to backdoor attacks. Prior backdoor attack methods typically rely on retraining with clean data or altering the model architecture, both of which can be resource-intensive and intrusive. In this paper, we propose Head-wise Pruning and Malicious Injection (HPMI), a novel retraining-free backdoor attack on transformers that does not alter the model's architecture. Our approach requires only a small subset of the original data and basic knowledge of the model architecture, eliminating the need for retraining the target transformer. Technically, HPMI works by pruning the least important head and injecting a pre-trained malicious head to establish the backdoor. We provide a rigorous theoretical justification demonstrating that the implanted backdoor resists detection and removal by state-of-the-art defense techniques, under reasonable assumptions. Experimental evaluations across multiple datasets further validate the effectiveness of HPMI, showing that it 1) incurs negligible clean accuracy loss, 2) achieves at least 99.55% attack success rate, and 3) bypasses four advanced defense mechanisms. Additionally, relative to state-of-the-art retraining-dependent attacks, HPMI achieves greater concealment and robustness against diverse defense strategies, while maintaining minimal impact on clean accuracy.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Head-wise Pruning and Malicious Injection (HPMI), a novel, retraining-free backdoor attack on transformer models. HPMI works by pruning a less important attention head within the transformer architecture and then injecting a pre-trained malicious head designed to activate upon the presence of a specific trigger. The authors provide theoretical justification and experimental validation demonstrating that HPMI achieves high attack success rates while maintaining acceptable clean accuracy, and resisting several defense mechanisms. HPMI offers a new way to backdoor transformer models without the computational overhead of retraining or model architecture modification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The retraining-free aspect is a key differentiator.  Existing backdoor attacks on transformers typically involve retraining the model, which is resource-intensive and detectable. HPMI's approach offers a more practical and stealthier attack vector. It modifies existing parameters.
*   **Theoretical Justification:**  The paper provides theoretical arguments explaining why HPMI is resistant to detection and removal by standard defenses.  This theoretical backing strengthens the claims made in the paper.
*   **Experimental Validation:** The experiments across multiple datasets and transformer architectures (ViT, DeiT, BERT) provide solid empirical evidence of HPMI's effectiveness.  The evaluation against multiple defense mechanisms enhances the credibility of the work.
*   **Practicality:** The reduced resource requirements (no retraining, limited data) make the attack feasible in real-world scenarios where adversaries may have limited resources or access.
* **Clarity**: The paper is well-structured and explains the HPMI technique clearly.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Trigger Dependence:**  Like many backdoor attacks, HPMI relies on specific triggers.  The effectiveness of the attack is directly linked to the robustness and stealthiness of the chosen trigger. While the authors use common triggers, a more thorough investigation of trigger design and resilience to adaptive defenses could strengthen the paper. This limitation is common for backdoors attacks.
*   **Attention Head Sensitivity**: The process of identifying the 'least important' head may not be universally applicable to all transformer architectures or tasks. Some transformers might be more sensitive to head pruning than others. Additionally, identifying the least important head requires an iterative evaluation which still demands resources.
*   **Scalability to Larger Models**: While the experiments cover common transformer sizes, the scalability of HPMI to extremely large language models (LLMs) is not fully addressed. The head pruning step might become more complex with the increased number of heads in these models.
*   **Defense Mechanisms**: While the paper tested with multiple defense mechanisms. In particular, STRIP and RAP have large room to improve, which the paper's results suggest their limitations. The paper could benefit from exploring more robust and adaptive defenses against HPMI.

**Significance:**

HPMI is a significant contribution to the field of backdoor attacks on transformers. It highlights a previously underexplored vulnerability and provides a practical and effective attack strategy. The retraining-free nature of the attack, coupled with its resilience to several defenses, makes it a serious threat. The paper will likely stimulate further research into more robust defense mechanisms and trigger mitigation strategies for transformers.

**Score:** 7.5

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-validated attack. The novelty of not requiring retraining is valuable. However, some of the weaknesses, such as potential architectural sensitivity and the need for triggers, prevent it from reaching a higher score. The practicality of the attack is significant, but further research on adaptive defenses and how to scale the approach on LLMs could enhance its impact. Considering both the strengths and weaknesses, a score of 7.5 accurately reflects the contribution of the paper.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Meta-Metrics and Best Practices for System-Level Inference Performance Benchmarking
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10251v1)
- **Authors**: Shweta Salaria, Zhuoran Liu, Nelson Mimura Gonzalez
- **Abstract**: Benchmarking inference performance (speed) of Foundation Models such as Large Language Models (LLM) involves navigating a vast experimental landscape to understand the complex interactions between hardware and software components. However, evaluating every possible test configuration is impractical, unfeasible and unnecessary. To address this challenge, we introduce FMwork, a comprehensive and methodical approach to creating a controlled testing environment that accurately reflects and characterizes performance. FMwork comprises a set of benchmkaring best practices with three key components: 1) meta-metrics, 2) parameter selection, and 3) strategic cost-performance evaluation. Meta-metrics account for time and resources spent on benchmarking and the relative accuracy of the results compared to a larger body of measurements, representing the complete experimental space. FMwork operationalizes the meta-metrics and provides efficient strategies for parameter selection and cost-performance analysis. Using the framework, we show up to 24x improvement (speedup and/or resource savings) running sweeps of experiments compared to the ground truth. Even already considering a subset of experiments as reference point (using the power of two for batch sizes), reducing experimental output size from 1024 to 128 tokens yields another 2.7x gain while keeping 96.6% accuracy for an evaluation using Llama 3.1 8B model.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FMwork, a benchmarking framework for evaluating the inference performance of large language models (LLMs).  It addresses the problem that exhaustive benchmarking of LLMs is computationally intractable due to the vast parameter space (model architecture, input/output sizes, batch size, precision, backends, etc.). FMwork proposes a methodology based on three key components: (1) meta-metrics to quantify the cost of benchmarking and the accuracy of the results relative to a larger sample space, (2) parameter selection strategies to optimize sampling efficiency, and (3) cost-performance analysis to quantify resource requirements. The framework also includes synthetic input/model generation to ensure representative workloads and flexible evaluation.  The authors demonstrate that FMwork can significantly reduce experimental time and resources (up to 24x improvement) while maintaining accuracy, using the Llama-3 8B model as a case study.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper addresses a very relevant and practical problem in the LLM field: the high cost of benchmarking. While the individual components, such as synthetic data generation and meta-metrics, aren't entirely novel in themselves, their combination within a comprehensive framework specifically designed for LLM inference benchmarking represents a significant contribution. The meta-metric approach, allowing the user to explicitly consider the cost/accuracy trade-off in the benchmarking process, is particularly innovative. The emphasis on cost-effectiveness and efficiency sets it apart from other benchmarking efforts that focus primarily on performance metrics. The idea of creating synthetic models designed to replicate the behaviour of real models and be benchmarked instead is also pretty interesting.

*   **Significance:** The framework has the potential to significantly impact how LLM inference is benchmarked and evaluated.  By providing a systematic and efficient methodology, FMwork enables researchers and practitioners to make informed decisions about hardware investments, model selection, and deployment strategies. The ability to reduce experimental costs by orders of magnitude while retaining reasonable accuracy is a major advantage. The open-source nature of FMwork enhances its accessibility and potential for adoption by the community. By explicitly modeling the time/resource/accuracy trade-off, FMwork could standardize LLM performance evaluation in the broader community, which is a problem that needs solving.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem of exhaustive benchmarking.
    *   The proposed framework is well-structured and comprehensive.
    *   The use of meta-metrics provides a quantitative way to assess the trade-off between cost and accuracy.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of FMwork in reducing experimental costs while maintaining accuracy.
    *   The discussion of synthetic input/model generation is valuable for controlled experimentation.
    *   The framework is open-source, facilitating adoption and further development.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the paper introduces synthetic model generation, its explanation is somewhat brief and it needs more investigation to really compare the capabilities with real models. A more in-depth evaluation of the synthetic models' fidelity in capturing the nuances of real model behavior (beyond throughput) would strengthen the paper.
    *   The accuracy metric, based on the normalized relative difference, could be improved with other more established statistical methods for performance comparison.
    *   While the results using the Llama-3 8B model are promising, the paper would be more compelling with evaluations using a wider range of models (larger models, different architectures) and hardware platforms.
    *   The focus is almost exclusively on inference, making limited references to other applications of LLMs such as fine-tuning. It is likely that such processes would also benefit from the same methodologies.
    *   The framework requires some technical expertise to set up and use effectively. A more user-friendly interface or a more extensive set of example use cases could lower the barrier to entry.

*   **Potential Influence:** FMwork has the potential to become a widely adopted benchmarking framework for LLM inference. It could influence the development of new LLM architectures and deployment strategies, driving innovation in the field. The emphasis on cost-effectiveness could also make LLMs more accessible to a wider range of users and organizations.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the LLM field by addressing the crucial issue of efficient benchmarking.  The novelty lies in the comprehensive framework combining meta-metrics, parameter selection, and synthetic data. The experimental results demonstrate the framework's effectiveness.  The open-source nature ensures accessibility.  However, the paper could be strengthened by a more in-depth analysis of the synthetic models, improved accuracy metrics, broader evaluations with different models and hardware, and improved user experience. Even with these limitations, the potential influence of FMwork is significant, justifying a score of 7.5. It is a clear step in the right direction, allowing a higher degree of automation in LLM benchmarking and taking into account all relevant factors.

- **Classification**: cs.PF
- **Score**: 7/10

### MRFD: Multi-Region Fusion Decoding with Self-Consistency for Mitigating Hallucinations in LVLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10264v1)
- **Authors**: Haonan Ge, Yiwei Wang, Ming-Hsuan Yang, Yujun Cai
- **Abstract**: Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have shown strong performance across multimodal tasks. However, they often produce hallucinations -- text that is inconsistent with visual input, due to the limited ability to verify information in different regions of the image. To address this, we propose Multi-Region Fusion Decoding (MRFD), a training-free decoding method that improves factual grounding by modeling inter-region consistency. MRFD identifies salient regions using cross-attention, generates initial responses for each, and computes reliability weights based on Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) among the responses. These weights guide a consistency-aware fusion of per-region predictions, using region-aware prompts inspired by Chain-of-Thought reasoning. Experiments across multiple LVLMs and benchmarks show that MRFD significantly reduces hallucinations and improves response factuality without requiring model updates.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "MRFD: Multi-Region Fusion Decoding with Self-Consistency for Mitigating Hallucinations in LVLMs" proposes a training-free decoding method, Multi-Region Fusion Decoding (MRFD), to reduce hallucinations in Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs). MRFD aims to improve factual grounding by modeling inter-region consistency. It identifies salient regions using cross-attention, generates initial responses for each, computes reliability weights based on Jensen-Shannon Divergence (JSD) among the responses, and then uses these weights to guide a consistency-aware fusion of per-region predictions using region-aware prompts inspired by Chain-of-Thought reasoning. Experiments across multiple LVLMs and benchmarks demonstrate that MRFD reduces hallucinations and improves response factuality without requiring model updates.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the specific combination of existing techniques (cross-attention, JSD divergence, CoT prompting) within a novel decoding strategy to address hallucinations in LVLMs. Individually, these techniques are well-established. However, the way MRFD leverages cross-attention to focus on salient regions, uses JSD to quantify inter-region consistency, and then fuses the region-specific information through CoT-inspired prompting represents a reasonably novel approach. The introduction of JSD for region consistency measurement is particularly interesting and shows a strong correlation with factual correctness.
*   **Significance:** Hallucinations are a major obstacle to the practical deployment of LVLMs. The paper's significance stems from its ability to reduce these hallucinations *without requiring model retraining*. The training-free nature of MRFD makes it easily applicable to existing LVLMs, which is a significant advantage. The experiments across multiple models (LLaVA-1.5 and InstructBLIP) and benchmarks (POPE, CHAIR, MME) provide strong evidence for the effectiveness and generalizability of the approach. The reported improvements in F1 score and CHAIR scores are quantitatively significant. The ablation studies provide a solid understanding of the contribution of each component of MRFD. The qualitative examples further illustrate the benefits of MRFD in producing more factually accurate and contextually relevant descriptions.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The approach is training-free, making it easy to implement on existing models.
    *   The use of JSD to measure inter-region consistency is well-motivated and empirically validated.
    *   The combination of cross-attention, JSD, and CoT prompting is effective in reducing hallucinations.
    *   The experimental results are thorough and demonstrate the generalizability of MRFD across multiple models and benchmarks.
    *   The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    *   Qualitative results further illustrate and validate the claims made.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The method relies on the quality of attention maps, which can be influenced by factors like architecture and input data. A failure of the attention mechanism would likely undermine the performance of MRFD.
    *   While the method is training-free, the choice of hyperparameters (number of regions K, temperature γ) could require some tuning for different models and tasks.
    *   The computational cost associated with analyzing multiple regions is higher than standard decoding. Although discussed, a more detailed analysis of runtime efficiency would be helpful in a practical setting.
    *   The paper primarily focuses on reducing hallucinations in image captioning and visual question answering tasks. It would be beneficial to see how MRFD performs in other multimodal tasks.
    *   The reliance on Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting techniques also introduces its limitations, with increased sensitivity to the prompts used.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's training-free approach has strong potential influence on the field. Other researchers might be inspired to develop similar decoding strategies that leverage inter-region consistency and avoid the need for expensive model retraining. The work could motivate future research on improving attention mechanisms in LVLMs, as the quality of attention maps directly affects the performance of MRFD.

**Justification for Score:**

The MRFD paper makes a solid contribution to the field of LVLMs by providing a simple yet effective training-free decoding method that significantly reduces hallucinations. While the individual techniques leveraged are not novel, their combination and specific application to this problem demonstrates creativity and practical value. The experiments are extensive and support the claims. However, the dependence on attention map quality and the potential tuning requirements are limitations. The contribution seems to be an incremental one on top of prior approaches. The reliance on CoT prompted fusion also poses limitations to the flexibility of the proposed approach. Overall, it makes a valuable contribution.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Why Cannot Large Language Models Ever Make True Correct Reasoning?
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10265v1)
- **Authors**: Jingde Cheng
- **Abstract**: Recently, with the application progress of AIGC tools based on large language models (LLMs), led by ChatGPT, many AI experts and more non-professionals are trumpeting the "understanding ability" and "reasoning ability" of the LLMs. The present author considers that the so-called "understanding ability" and "reasoning ability" of LLMs are just illusions of those people who with vague concepts. In fact, the LLMs can never have the true understanding ability and true reasoning ability. This paper intents to explain that, because the essential limitations of their working principle, the LLMs can never have the ability of true correct reasoning.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper "Why Cannot Large Language Models Ever Make True Correct Reasoning?"

**Summary:**

The paper argues that Large Language Models (LLMs) cannot achieve true correct reasoning due to their fundamental limitations. The author criticizes the commonly touted "understanding ability" and "reasoning ability" of LLMs as illusions, asserting that they lack true comprehension and reasoning. The paper defines "correct reasoning" as drawing new conclusions from given premises, providing conclusive relevant evidence. The core argument revolves around the fact that LLMs, based on statistical distributions and lacking a built-in mechanism for correctness evaluation based on formal logic (specifically strong relevant logic), can only simulate reasoning forms without ensuring logical validity. The paper emphasizes the importance of correctness evaluation in reasoning and concludes that the current architecture of LLMs, being inherently statistical and token-based, cannot incorporate the necessary logical framework for guaranteed correct reasoning. The paper advocates for the use of strong relevant logic to assess the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's core argument, that LLMs fundamentally lack the capacity for true correct reasoning due to their reliance on statistical patterns rather than formal logic, is not entirely novel. This idea has been expressed in varying forms by other researchers examining the limitations of LLMs, especially their tendency towards hallucination and generation of factually incorrect information. However, the paper's strength lies in its clear and direct assertion that LLMs are inherently incapable of true reasoning. The paper's distinction of "correct reasoning," while not new in philosophy, is novel in the context of evaluating LLMs.

The paper's novelty also lies in explicitly connecting the limitations of LLMs to the lack of a built-in, dynamic correctness evaluation mechanism rooted in a strong relevant logic. The proposal that the working principle and architecture of LLMs cannot incorporate such a mechanism is also a contribution.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from its potential to shift the focus of LLM research. It challenges the prevalent narrative of LLMs as possessing emergent reasoning abilities and urges researchers to address the fundamental limitations in their architecture rather than merely focusing on superficial improvements. Highlighting the need for correctness evaluation criteria in the discussion about LLMs is important, since much of the research focuses on "reasoning" without rigor. The paper calls for a serious re-evaluation of what LLMs are doing.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Definitions:** The paper provides a well-defined notion of correct reasoning, establishing a specific benchmark for evaluating LLM performance.
*   **Emphasis on Formal Logic:** The paper's advocacy for strong relevant logic as the foundation for true reasoning is grounded in established philosophical principles.
*   **Direct Argument:** The paper makes a bold and uncompromising claim, which encourages critical discussion about LLM capabilities.
*   **Well-Organized Structure:** The paper is structured logically, building its argument step by step from definitions to limitations.
*   **Comprehensive Reasoning:** The paper is thorough, and references extensively previous studies on topics such as the history of logics.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Lack of Empirical Validation:** The paper is primarily theoretical. While it makes a strong case against LLM reasoning capabilities, it doesn't present new empirical evidence to support its claims. Providing specific examples of LLM failures that illustrate the limitations of their statistical approach could strengthen the argument.
*   **Limited Discussion of Alternative Approaches:** While the paper champions strong relevant logic, it could benefit from a more thorough discussion of alternative approaches to incorporating logic or reasoning mechanisms into LLMs. This would provide a more balanced perspective.
